# BMAD Output Full Review Notes — Homefront + LLM-Wiki
Generated from uploaded `_bmad-output` archives. macOS `._*` resource-fork files and `.DS_Store` files were excluded.
## Reviewed File Counts
- Homefront: 102 real files
- LLM-Wiki: 59 real files

## Cross-Repo Synthesis
- The current planning/doc set is not internally consistent enough to implement safely without a shared contract.
- Homefront is planned as the household/cell operating runtime, but its BMAD artifacts still use `household_id` everywhere and mix old/new memory terminology.
- Honcho should be the profile/assistant/session memory layer using Honcho V3-style `workspace`, `peer`, `session`, and `message` terminology.
- LLM-Wiki is currently planned and implemented as a daemon-governed page/domain/wiki service. It does not yet have Homefront's deterministic structured facts API.
- Homefront Epic 5 currently overuses Honcho for shared routine facts; that needs correction: stable deterministic facts belong in LLM-Wiki, not Honcho.
- LLM-Wiki Epic H added Honcho push/pull, but that is a bridge/integration feature, not a replacement for Homefront's context assembler and policy runtime.
- The product should not use `MVP` or partial/rough release language. Use `Office Pilot` as the controlled self-test gate and `Family Production Rollout` as the required readiness target.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/1-7-default-unknown-users-to-guest-limited-mode.md`
- Size: 8472 bytes
- Title: Story 1.7: Default Unknown Users to Guest/Limited Mode
- Status signals: review; review
- Headings: # Story 1.7: Default Unknown Users to Guest/Limited Mode | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ### Architecture Guardrails | ### UX Guardrails | ### Latest Technical Information
- Relevant keyword hits: 40
- Acceptance criteria preview: L15: 1. Given a request reaches the Homefront cell without a resolved household profile, when the system evaluates the request context, then it treats the requester as guest/limited, and it does not grant adult/admin, child-private, support/operator, or household-wide permissions.; L16: 2. Given an unknown or unresolved user opens the PWA, when the shell loads, then the user sees a limited experience, and no private household routine state, memory, transcripts, integration credentials, or admin controls are shown.; L17: 3. Given an unknown or unresolved voice/chat identity submits an action request, when the request would affect household setup, people, assistants, permissions, memory, integrations, or risky actions, then the system denies or asks for clarification according to the limited-mode policy, and it does not execute the action silently.; L18: 4. Given a limited-mode decision is made, when the backend returns the outcome, then the response clearly identifies the request as limited or unresolved, and the frontend renders a plain household-language explanation without exposing policy internals.; L19: 5. Given a household admin later associates the user with a valid profile, when the user opens Homefront again, then the shell resolves the user's assigned profile and role, and limited-mode restrictions no longer apply beyond that profile's normal permissions.

Given a household admin later associates the user with a valid profile, when the user opens Homefront again, then the shell resolves the user's assigned profile and role, and limited-mode restrictions no longer apply beyond that profile's normal permissions.; L25:   - [x] Add a default guest/limited request context when no household profile resolves.

- Title: Story 1.8: Setup Readiness Path to Routine Setup
- Status signals: review; review

- Acceptance criteria preview: L15: 1. Given required setup items are incomplete, when the adult/admin views the setup shell, then Homefront shows what is missing in plain household language, and provides direct actions to complete each missing item.; L17: 3. Given at least three household members are configured for participation, when readiness is evaluated, then Homefront marks the household as ready for founder validation participation needs, and if fewer than three are configured, it shows that this is a validation readiness gap rather than a hard system failure.; L19: 5. Given readiness status is returned by the backend, when the frontend renders setup progress, then it uses service-owned readiness state, and it does not infer setup readiness through frontend-only checks.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/2-91-oidc-compatible-auth-foundation.md`
- Size: 20090 bytes
- Title: Story 2.91: OIDC-Compatible Auth Foundation
- Status signals: review; review
- Headings: # Story 2.91: OIDC-Compatible Auth Foundation | ## Story | ## Context & Motivation | ## Prerequisites | ## Net-New Components | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes
- Relevant keyword hits: 64
- Acceptance criteria preview: L29: 1. Given a request arrives at any protected endpoint, when no valid Bearer token is present, then the API returns `401 Unauthorized` with a structured error body—never a 200 or a policy decision based on missing identity.; L31: 2. Given a client presents a valid Bearer JWT, when the middleware verifies the token's signature, expiry, issuer, and audience claims, then `AuthContext` is injected into the request with resolved `user_id`, `household_id`, `role`, and `profile_id`—and the `X-Dev-User-*` header path is unreachable.; L33: 3. Given the `DEV_AUTH_ENABLED` flag is set to `true` in `.env`, when a dev token is issued via the `/auth/dev/token` endpoint (only reachable when flag is true), then the endpoint issues a properly signed JWT with the requested claims—it does not bypass verification. The verification path is identical in dev and production.; L35: 4. Given a JWT is within 60 seconds of expiry, when the client calls `/auth/refresh`, then a new JWT is issued and the old token is invalidated in the token denylist, and the new expiry is recorded in the session store.; L37: 5. Given a client calls `/auth/logout`, when the server processes the request, then the current token is added to the token denylist, all other sessions for that user can optionally be revoked, and subsequent requests using the invalidated token return `401`.

This is not a "dev shortcut"—it is a complete absence of authentication. Any client (or attacker) can claim any role by setting a header. Given a record is created or updated in any service, when the ORM flushes the change, then `created_at` and `updated_at` are set by the database engine (PostgreSQL `NOW()` server default / trigger), not by the application layer—there is no application-side timestamp assignment for these fields.; L43: 5. Given two concurrent requests update the same domain record simultaneously, when both transactions commit, then optimistic concurrency control detects the conflict: the second writer receives a `ConflictError` (HTTP 409), not a silent overwrite.

Given an approval request transitions to any terminal state (`approved`, `denied`, `left_waiting`, `expired`), when the transition completes, then the system creates an in-app `Notification` record for the requester (and for admins in the case of `expired`) with the decision, a plain-language explanation, and a link to the relevant approval context.; L48: 3. Given a profile calls `GET /ledger/entries` or `GET /ledger/entries/{entry_id}`, when the authorization check runs, then:; L49: - `household_admin` and `adult_parent` roles can read all entries for their household.; L50: - `child` role can read only ledger entries where `requester_profile_id = their own profile_id` and `entry_type NOT IN ('approval_decision', 'policy_override')`.

- Key lines preview: L1: # Story 2.94: Approval Lifecycle & Ledger Hardening; L7: As a household admin,; L8: I want approval requests to expire gracefully with escalation, to reach me via notification, and for activity records to be readable only by authorized household roles,; L9: so that pending approvals never silently stall, I am alerted before they expire, and ledger records are not accessible to the profiles whose actions they describe.; L13: Three gaps undermine the approval and audit systems built in Stories 2.3 and 2.4:; L15: 1. **Approval queue has no time boundary.** An approval-routed risky action can sit in `pending` indefinitely. There is no TTL, no escalation trigger, and no ledger record when an approval expires without a decision. A household where the admin is unavailable for a day will accumulate stale pending approvals with no notification and no automatic resolution.; L17: 2. **No notification path exists.** The approval queue relies on the admin noticing the queue badge in the PWA. There is no event, no in-app alert, and no extensible notification hook. For a safety-critical system (a pending lock-unlock action, for example), this is a design gap.; L19: 3. **Ledger has no read access control.** `LedgerService.list_entries()` and `get_entry()` are not gated by role or household membership. A child profile could enumerate all ledger entries—including policy denials, approval decisions, and safety classifications for their own actions. The append-only nature of the ledger is correctly enforced; the read path is not.

- Acceptance criteria preview: L44: 1. Given any API endpoint receives more requests from a single `household_id` than the configured rate limit within the sliding window, when the limit is exceeded, then the API returns `429 Too Many Requests` with `Retry-After` header, and the throttled request is not processed. Rate limits are enforced per household and per profile separately, with stricter limits for action-execution and policy-evaluation endpoints.; L46: 2. Given a state-changing request (POST, PATCH, DELETE) arrives at any endpoint that is not the auth token endpoint, when the request has no valid `X-CSRF-Token` header or the token does not match the session's double-submit cookie value, then the API returns `403 Forbidden` with `{"error": "csrf_invalid"}`. SPA tokens are issued at shell-context load and rotated on login.; L48: 3. Given the `support_operator` role is documented in `docs/appendices/role-duty-map.md` and enforced in code, when a support_operator token is used, then the operator can read: household metadata, profile names and roles (not private profile data), shell-context readiness state, ledger entry types/outcomes (payload redacted — enforced by Story 2.94), and approval queue status. The operator cannot: read routine memory, read private context, make approval decisions, trigger actions, or access any data outside their assigned household scope.; L50: 4. Given `SUPPORT_ACCESS_ENABLED = true` and a support_operator reads any household resource, when the access is processed, then a `support_access_log` entry is written with: `operator_profile_id`, `accessed_resource_type`, `accessed_resource_id`, `access_at`, `request_id`, `household_id`. This log is append-only and not accessible to the support_operator themselves.; L52: 5. Given an admin configures a wake name, when the name is submitted for validation, then the system rejects names that appear in the maintained `WAKE_NAME_DENYLIST` (file-backed, hot-reloadable), which includes: common English words under 4 characters, English given names with global frequency rank in the top 500 (sourced from a vendored name frequency list), names phonetically similar to existing household wake names (same Soundex code via `jellyfish.soundex()`), and names identical to existing assistant wake names in the same household. Rejection returns a structured error with `{"error": "wake_name_rejected", "reason": "too_common|phonetic_conflict|household_conflict", "suggestion": null_or_string}`.

Endpoint classes: `auth` (loose), `read` (moderate), `action` (strict), `policy_eval` (strict). Store bucket state in PostgreSQL `rate_limit_buckets` table (for Phase 1; replace with Redis when multi-instance deployment begins).; L61: - [x] `rate_limit_buckets` table: `key VARCHAR(255) PK`, `tokens FLOAT NOT NULL`, `last_refill_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL`. Refill logic: `tokens = min(capacity, tokens + (elapsed_seconds * refill_rate))`.; L62: - [x] Platform defaults (configurable via env vars — all must be present in `.env.example`):

A misbehaving extension, compromised child device, or external attacker faces no throttling. For a system that logs every action to a ledger, unbounded request rates can also drive unbounded ledger growth.; L19: 3. **No defined support_operator permission boundary**: The `support_operator` role is introduced in Story 1.3 and gated by `SUPPORT_ACCESS_ENABLED`. What it can read and why is never documented in code or tests. A support operator's access to household data — including child profiles, private context, and approval history — is a privacy risk with no explicit scope definition.; L23: These are platform-layer concerns that must be handled before the system is used by a real household. ### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/2-97-frontend-resilience-and-ux-correctness.md`
- Size: 21614 bytes
- Title: Story 2.97: Frontend Resilience & UX Correctness
- Status signals: completed; completed
- Headings: # Story 2.97: Frontend Resilience & UX Correctness | ## Story | ## Context & Motivation | ## Prerequisites | ## Net-New Components | ## Key Environment Facts | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks
- Relevant keyword hits: 43
- Acceptance criteria preview: L41: 1. Given the PWA is loaded and the API returns a network error or 5xx response, when any data-fetching component receives the error, then it renders an `ErrorBoundary` fallback UI that shows the error type, a retry button, and the last known good state (stale data with a staleness indicator) rather than crashing or rendering blank.; L43: 2. Given the device loses network connectivity while the PWA is open, when the browser's `navigator.onLine` transitions to `false`, then the PWA displays a persistent offline banner, disables all write-action controls (approve, deny, submit) with a tooltip explaining why they are disabled, and queues no requests silently—it is explicit that actions cannot be submitted offline.; L45: 3. Given the API returns a `label_key` string (e.g., `"readiness.missing_profiles.label"`), when the frontend resolves the string for display, then it looks it up in a locale bundle (`en-US.json` minimum) and renders the human-readable string. If the key is not found in the bundle, it renders `[label_key]` (the key wrapped in brackets) as a visible sentinel—never the raw key as if it were display text.; L47: 4. Given any approval action button (Approve, Deny, Leave Waiting) is rendered in the `ApprovalsView`, when the component mounts, then each button's click target is at minimum 44×44px (using Tailwind `h-11 min-w-[44px]` or equivalent), keyboard-focusable, and has a non-color-only indicator of its semantic meaning (icon + text, not color alone).; L49: 5. Given the backend's `GET /notifications` returns unread notifications, when the PWA shell header renders, then it shows a notification badge with the unread count (capped at "99+"), and clicking it opens a notification drawer listing each notification with type, title, body, and a mark-read action.
- Task preview: L57: - [x] Add error boundary infrastructure (AC: 1, 2, 6); L58: - [x] Create `apps/web/src/components/error/ErrorBoundary.tsx`: React class component (required for `componentDidCatch`); accepts `fallback: (error, reset) => ReactNode` prop; renders `fallback` on error, children when healthy.; L59: - [x] Create `apps/web/src/components/error/ApiErrorFallback.tsx`: standard API error fallback UI — error message, retry button, optional stale data slot.; L60: - [x] Wrap all top-level route components in `ErrorBoundary` in `App.tsx`.; L61: - [x] Create `apps/web/src/hooks/useApiQuery.ts`: thin wrapper over `fetch()` (or `@tanstack/react-query` if already in use) that: sets `AbortController` timeout (5s default, configurable per call), handles `offline` state before fetching, returns `{data, error, isLoading, isStale, retry}`.
- Key lines preview: L7: As a household member,; L9: so that the app remains usable when connectivity is degraded and every interaction meets basic accessibility standards.; L15: 1. **No error boundary or offline behavior**: The PWA is mobile-first and may operate on household Wi-Fi with intermittent connectivity. No component handles `fetch()` failures, timeouts, or `503` responses. An API outage renders the PWA blank or crashed with an uncaught promise rejection. Story 1.3.5 identified non-cancellable state machines as a problem; those were not fully resolved.; L17: 2. **`label_key` pattern with no i18n infrastructure**: Story 1.8 introduced `label_key` fields in API responses (e.g., `ReadinessItem.label_key = "readiness.profile_count.label"`) intended for frontend localization. The PWA receives these keys and has no lookup mechanism to resolve them to display strings. Every `label_key` field renders as a raw key string or causes a runtime error.; L19: 3. **Approval touch targets are 36px**: Story 2.3 completion notes acknowledge `h-9` (36px Tailwind) for approval decision buttons. The project's own UX specification requires 44px minimum for critical actions. Approve/Deny/Leave Waiting are the most critical buttons in the approval flow. This is a known accessibility failure in the self-declared passing AC4.; L21: 4. **No PWA-level notification indicator**: Story 2.94 adds a `Notification` model and `GET /notifications` endpoint. The PWA has no component to show unread notification count or a notification drawer. This renders the entire approval escalation notification flow invisible to the user.; L28: - Stories 2.91–2.93 affect the backend but do not block frontend work. The frontend uses the existing dev auth header until 2.91's token flow is integrated into the PWA (out of scope for this story — the PWA auth integration is a separate concern).; L34: ## Key Environment Facts

- Relevant keyword hits: 29
- Acceptance criteria preview: L14: 1. Given a simulated routine contains integration actions (e.g., `lock.unlock`, `light.turn_on`), when simulation runs, then each action is evaluated against the Epic 2 policy and risk classification engine — the simulation produces per-action policy outcomes: `allowed`, `approval_required`, `denied`.; L15: 2. Given a simulated action is classified as `approval_required` (e.g., `lock.unlock`), when the simulation result is shown, then the admin sees which approver the action would route to and under what policy condition — expressed in household-language, not internal policy syntax.; L16: 3. Given a simulated action is `denied` by policy, when the simulation result is shown, then the admin sees the denial reason in household-language and a prompt to either remove the action or adjust the policy before activation.; L17: 4. Given a simulated routine has only `allowed` actions, when simulation passes, then no policy issues appear in the result and the routine is clear for activation consideration.; L18: 5. Given policy outcomes are simulated, when the simulation result is written, then a single summary ledger entry is created typed `simulation` — not per-action policy evaluation entries — so that real policy enforcement audit entries are never generated for simulated runs.

- Acceptance criteria preview: L16: 1. Given llm-wiki is a custom-built external system, when the cell runtime needs to read or write household knowledge, then it communicates with llm-wiki via a defined API contract (REST or MCP), and the cell runtime does not store canonical knowledge facts locally in its own schema.; L17: 2. Given household knowledge categories are defined, when facts are stored, then the knowledge base supports: household roster (members, roles, relationships), recurring assignments (who walks the dogs, who handles school drop-off), pet/animal data, appliance make/model/location, and structured household preferences — each in a queryable, typed structure.; L18: 3. Given a knowledge fact changes in a source system (e.g. calendar update, manual admin edit), when a hook or API call notifies the cell runtime, then the cell runtime updates the corresponding llm-wiki knowledge entry, and outdated facts do not silently persist.; L19: 4. Given the simulation engine (Story 3.7) needs a context snapshot, when it requests one, then the cell runtime assembles a context snapshot from llm-wiki knowledge entries relevant to the household and routine, and the snapshot is a point-in-time read — not a live reference.; L20: 5. Given llm-wiki is unavailable, when the cell runtime attempts a knowledge read, then it fails gracefully with a `DegradedContextSignal` (source: `llm_wiki`) rather than a hard error, and simulation and routine evaluation can proceed with reduced context when policy allows.

Given a knowledge fact changes in a source system (e.g. calendar update, manual admin edit), when a hook or API call notifies the cell runtime, then the cell runtime updates the corresponding llm-wiki knowledge entry, and outdated facts do not silently persist.; L19: 4. Given the simulation engine (Story 3.7) needs a context snapshot, when it requests one, then the cell runtime assembles a context snapshot from llm-wiki knowledge entries relevant to the household and routine, and the snapshot is a point-in-time read — not a live reference.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-3-honcho-memory-integration.md`

- Status signals: ready-for-dev; ready-for-dev

- Relevant keyword hits: 34
- Acceptance criteria preview: L16: 1. Given a profile exists with an assigned personal assistant, when the Honcho integration is initialized, then each profile's assistant has an isolated Honcho app/session scoped to that profile — no cross-profile memory leakage.; L17: 2. Given an AI assistant interaction occurs, when the interaction completes, then relevant context is written to the profile's Honcho session, and that context is retrievable in subsequent interactions for the same profile.; L18: 3. Given the simulation engine or routine evaluation needs assistant context, when context is requested, then the cell runtime reads from the appropriate profile's Honcho session and includes relevant memories in the context snapshot alongside llm-wiki knowledge.; L19: 4. Given Honcho is unavailable, when the cell runtime attempts a memory read or write, then it fails gracefully with a `DegradedContextSignal` (source: `honcho`) — the interaction proceeds without memory context rather than failing entirely, and the signal is logged and included in the context snapshot.; L20: 5. Given Honcho contains personal memory, when a profile is deleted, then the admin is offered the option to export the profile's Honcho session data as a portable JSON file before deletion proceeds. If the admin exports, the file is generated and made available for download. Whether or not the export is taken, the profile's Honcho session is hard-deleted on confirmation. The export format is self-describing and usable by another Homefront instance or a third-party system.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-4-integration-credential-and-token-lifecycle-foundation.md`
- Size: 13510 bytes
- Title: Story 3.4: Integration Credential and Token Lifecycle Foundation
- Status signals: ready-for-dev; ready-for-dev
- Headings: # Story 3.4: Integration Credential and Token Lifecycle Foundation | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ## References | ## Dev Agent Record | ### Agent Model Used
- Relevant keyword hits: 39
- Acceptance criteria preview: L13: 1. Given an integration needs to store credentials, when credentials are saved, then they are encrypted at rest, stored cell-local (never sent to the control plane), and keyed by household + integration type + credential type.; L14: 2. Given OAuth2 credentials are stored, when the credential service is called to retrieve a token, then it checks whether the token expires within 5 minutes and refreshes it inline before returning — if refresh fails, the caller receives a degraded-context signal and the integration state transitions to `expired`, triggering an admin notification.; L15: 3. Given an API key or long-lived token is stored (e.g. Home Assistant), when it is written, then it is stored under the same credential store as OAuth2 tokens using a non-expiring credential type — the same read/write interface is used regardless of credential type.; L16: 4. Given an integration's credential state changes, when the state is read, then the integration state model returns one of: `connected`, `expired`, `revoked`, `degraded`, `error`, `disconnected` — with a human-readable reason where applicable.; L17: 5. Given a new integration is added in a future story, when it registers with the credential foundation, then it provides an integration type key, credential type(s), and optional refresh handler — no changes to core credential store logic are required.
- Task preview: L21: - [ ] Add `integration_credentials` table: `household_id`, `integration_type`, `credential_type`, `encrypted_value`, `expires_at` (nullable), `state`, `updated_at` — with a unique constraint on `(household_id, integration_type, credential_type)` enforced at the database level (not only in application code) to prevent duplicate credential rows for the same integration (AC: 1); L22: - [ ] Add encryption at rest using AES-256-GCM with key from `CELL_CREDENTIAL_ENCRYPTION_KEY` env var (AC: 1); L23: - [ ] Add startup validation: fail fast if `CELL_CREDENTIAL_ENCRYPTION_KEY` is missing or not a valid 64-character hex string (AC: 1); L24: - [ ] Add `CELL_CREDENTIAL_ENCRYPTION_KEY=` entry to `.env.example` with: (a) generation command (`python -c "import secrets; print(secrets.token_hex(32))"`), (b) explicit key-loss warning ("If this key is lost, all stored integration credentials are permanently unrecoverable — you must re-enter all credentials"), and (c) explicit key-rotation warning ("Key rotation requires a manual re-encryption migration of all rows in integration_credentials — there is no automated rotation in v1. Do not change this key without running the migration first") (AC: 1); L25: - [ ] Add key backup acknowledgment endpoint: `POST /api/settings/credential-key-backup-confirmed` — records in `household_settings` that the admin has confirmed they have backed up `CELL_CREDENTIAL_ENCRYPTION_KEY`. This is a boolean flag only; the key itself is never sent over the wire.

- Acceptance criteria preview: L14: 1. Given Google Calendar is connected, when the cell runtime reads calendar events, then it requests the minimum required OAuth2 scope (`calendar.readonly`), reads upcoming events for the relevant household profiles, and writes schedule-relevant facts (presence, conflicts, day patterns) to llm-wiki via the knowledge foundation (Story 3.2).; L16: 3. Given calendar data changes (new event, cancelled event, schedule swap), when the update is detected via polling or webhook, then the corresponding llm-wiki knowledge entries are updated and any affected routine draft versions are incremented.; L17: 4. Given Google Calendar is unavailable or disconnected, when the simulation engine requests calendar context, then the context snapshot includes a `DegradedContextSignal` (source: `google_calendar`), simulation and routine evaluation proceed using saved llm-wiki facts where policy allows, and the user sees a clear unavailability message rather than a silent gap.

- Relevant keyword hits: 24
- Acceptance criteria preview: L14: 1. Given a pluggable device integration abstraction is defined, when Home Assistant is configured, then it is implemented as the first integration against that abstraction — device discovery, state read, action execution, and event subscription are all mediated through the abstraction interface, not HA-specific code in the routine engine.; L15: 2. Given an adult/admin configures Home Assistant, when they provide the HA URL and a long-lived API token, then credentials are stored using the credential foundation (Story 3.4) under the `home_assistant` integration type, and the token is never returned via API after storage.; L16: 3. Given Home Assistant is connected, when device state is read, then the cell runtime uses the HA REST API and reflects device state in the routine simulation and context snapshot.; L17: 4. Given an action is executed via Home Assistant, when the action is requested, then it is narrow, allowlisted, risk-classified per Epic 2 policy, policy-gated, and audit-logged — no unrestricted HA control is permitted.; L18: 5. Given Home Assistant is unavailable or the token is invalid, when the integration state is checked, then it reflects `degraded` or `error`, affected routine steps are marked accordingly, and non-device routine tasks can continue when policy allows.

The `subscribe_events` method is defined in the interface for future implementations but is not exercised in v1 — the `HomeAssistantIntegration` implementation must raise `NotImplementedError` with message "Event subscription is not implemented in v1. Use polling for state." Do not return silently or return a no-op callable — a silent stub would hide the gap when a future caller expects subscription to work. (AC: 1, 6); L24: - [ ] Implement `HomeAssistantIntegration(DeviceIntegration)` under `integrations/device/home_assistant.py` (AC: 1-3); L25: - [ ] Register `home_assistant` in the credential registry (Story 3.4) with API key credential type (AC: 2); L26: - [ ] Add HA connection setup: URL + long-lived token input, validation, state check (AC: 2-3); L27: - [ ] Add device action allowlist: defined action types with risk classification (AC: 4)

- Relevant keyword hits: 26
- Acceptance criteria preview: L21: 1. Given a routine draft exists, when an admin opens the flow editor, then they see a block-canvas (n8n-style, using React Flow/XYFlow) with the following block types: task, condition, integration action, and participant assignment — blocks are connected by edges that define routing.; L22: 2. Given an admin adds a task block, when they configure it, then they can set: assignee (household profile), time window (start offset, duration), description, and one or more conditions that reference llm-wiki facts or platform context (e.g., "only if kids are home", "skip if school holiday").; L23: 3. Given an admin saves a draft, when a concurrent save conflict is detected via optimistic locking (draft version mismatch), then the admin sees a warning identifying which fields differ, and must explicitly confirm before overwriting.; L24: 4. Given a draft is saved, when the draft version increments, then a background job automatically runs simulation for the new version — simulation runs silently on pass, notifies admin on failure.; L25: 5. Given an admin triggers simulation manually or it is triggered automatically, when simulation runs, then the cell runtime assembles a context snapshot (llm-wiki facts + Honcho memory + integration states + platform settings), routes tasks to participants, evaluates conditions, and produces a typed `simulation` result record with: pass/fail, per-task outcomes, unresolved items, and a stable result ID.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-7c-auto-simulation-and-ledger.md`
- Size: 7604 bytes
- Title: Story 3.7c: Auto-Simulation Job and Ledger Entry
- Status signals: ready-for-dev; ready-for-dev
- Headings: # Story 3.7c: Auto-Simulation Job and Ledger Entry | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ## References | ## Dev Agent Record | ### Agent Model Used
- Relevant keyword hits: 17
- Acceptance criteria preview: L14: 1. Given a routine draft version increments (via any save — UI or API), when the version is committed, then a background job enqueues a simulation run for that draft version using the same simulation engine as manual triggers (Story 3.7a) — no separate code path. If a simulation job for an earlier version of the same routine is already queued and has not yet started, it is cancelled before the new job is enqueued — at most one pending simulation job exists per routine at any time.; L15: 2. Given the auto-simulation job runs, when simulation passes, then no notification is sent and the result is silently available in the simulation result panel (Story 3.7b).; L16: 3. Given the auto-simulation job runs, when simulation fails (unresolved conditions, blocked tasks, engine error), then an admin notification is created identifying the routine name and the failure reason in household language.; L17: 4. Given a simulation run completes (auto or manual), when the result is written to `routine_simulation_results`, then a single summary ledger entry is also written typed `simulation` — containing the routine ID, draft version, simulation result ID, and pass/fail status. No per-task or per-action ledger entries are written for simulated runs.; L18: 5. Given a simulation ledger entry exists, when it is read, then it is clearly distinguishable from real action ledger entries by its `simulation` type — it must never be counted in policy enforcement metrics or approval queues.
- Task preview: L22: - [ ] Add background job: triggered on draft version increment, enqueues simulation run (AC: 1); L23: - [ ] Add `pending_simulation_job_id` nullable column to `routines` table (AC: 1); L24: - [ ] Add job deduplication: use `SELECT FOR UPDATE` on the routine row within a transaction — if `pending_simulation_job_id` is non-null, cancel the existing job by ID, then enqueue the new job and write its ID to `pending_simulation_job_id`, then commit. This makes concurrent enqueue attempts for the same routine fully atomic. Clear `pending_simulation_job_id` on job completion or cancellation (AC: 1); L25: - [ ] Wire job to use the same simulation engine as manual trigger — no parallel implementation (AC: 1); L26: - [ ] Add silent-pass path: no notification, result written to `routine_simulation_results` (AC: 2)

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-8-build-morning-routine-via-ui.md`

- Title: Story 3.8: Build Morning Routine via UI

Given the editor is open, when the admin builds the morning routine, then the routine exercises all block types: at least two task blocks with participant assignments, at least one condition block referencing a llm-wiki fact (e.g., "kids are home"), at least one integration action block (Home Assistant or Google Calendar), and at least one time window with offset from routine start.; L16: 3. Given the routine includes a Home Assistant integration action, when the block is configured, then it references a device from the HA device list (Story 3.6), selects an allowlisted action, and shows the risk classification for that action.; L17: 4. Given the routine includes a condition that references a Google Calendar fact, when the block is configured, then it references a llm-wiki knowledge entry populated by the calendar integration (Story 3.5) — the condition is not hardcoded but reads from llm-wiki.; L18: 5. Given the routine draft is saved, when auto-simulation runs (Story 3.7c), then the simulation produces a result covering all tasks and conditions in the routine, and the admin can view per-task outcomes in the editor simulation panel (Story 3.7b).

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-9-build-morning-routine-via-ai-tooling.md`

- Status signals: ready-for-dev; ready-for-dev

- Acceptance criteria preview: L14: 1. Given a structured routine schema is defined, when an AI assistant calls the routine API or MCP tool, then it can: read an existing routine draft, create a new routine draft, add or modify blocks (tasks, conditions, integration actions, participant assignments), and trigger simulation — using the same data structures as the visual editor.; L15: 2. Given the AI creates or modifies a routine, when the routine is saved, then it goes through the same draft versioning, optimistic locking, and auto-simulation pipeline as UI-created routines — no separate code path exists for AI-authored routines.; L16: 3. Given the morning routine from Story 3.8 exists, when the AI assistant reads it via the routine API/MCP, then it can describe the routine in natural language, identify which conditions are unresolved, and propose modifications.; L17: 4. Given the AI proposes a modification to the morning routine (e.g., add a new task, change a condition, swap a participant), when the modification is applied via the API/MCP tool, then the draft version increments and auto-simulation runs.; L18: 5. Given the AI triggers simulation, when the simulation result is returned, then the AI can interpret the per-task outcomes and report them in natural language — identifying blocked tasks, unresolved conditions, and approval-required actions.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/4-7-adult-live-routine-status-and-manual-resolution.md`
- Size: 4892 bytes
- Title: Story 4.7: Adult Live Routine Status and Manual Resolution
- Status signals: ready-for-dev; ready-for-dev
- Headings: # Story 4.7: Adult Live Routine Status and Manual Resolution | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ## References | ## Dev Agent Record | ### Agent Model Used
- Relevant keyword hits: 21
- Acceptance criteria preview: L15: 1. Given a live routine is active, when an adult/admin opens live status, then Homefront shows current step states, waiting items, missed items, escalations, and blocked or degraded steps.; L16: 2. Given the adult/admin manually resolves, skips, or corrects a step, when the action is permitted by role, policy, and step permissions, then Homefront updates routine state, and appends a ledger record for the correction or manual outcome.; L17: 3. Given live routine status is shown in the PWA, when step states render, then status labels are paired with text or icon cues, and raw internal state names are not exposed to family-facing users.; L18: 4. Given an adult participant opens routine status, when they have assigned routine items, then they can view and act on their own assigned items, and admin-only controls remain hidden unless that participant has admin permission.; L19: 5. Given an active routine has prior activation snapshots (from Story 3.12's ring buffer), when an admin opens the routine settings, then they can view the list of available snapshots (version, activation timestamp, activating user) and select one to roll back to.
- Task preview: L25: - [ ] Add Alembic migration: add `inactive` to the `routines.status` enum (`draft | active | inactive`) — Story 3.12 shipped with only `draft | active`; this migration extends it (AC: 2); L26: - [ ] Add routine deactivation endpoint: transitions status from `active` to `inactive`, writes a ledger entry typed `deactivation`, blocks if a live run is currently in progress (AC: 2); L27: - [ ] Add adult/admin live status endpoint and UI (AC: 1, 3-4); L28: - [ ] Add manual resolve/skip/correction operations with role, policy, and step permission checks (AC: 2, 4); L29: - [ ] Append ledger records for corrections and manual outcomes (AC: 2)
- Key lines preview: L1: # Story 4.7: Adult Live Routine Status and Manual Resolution; L10: I want to inspect and correct a live routine run,; L15: 1. Given a live routine is active, when an adult/admin opens live status, then Homefront shows current step states, waiting items, missed items, escalations, and blocked or degraded steps.; L16: 2. Given the adult/admin manually resolves, skips, or corrects a step, when the action is permitted by role, policy, and step permissions, then Homefront updates routine state, and appends a ledger record for the correction or manual outcome.; L17: 3. Given live routine status is shown in the PWA, when step states render, then status labels are paired with text or icon cues, and raw internal state names are not exposed to family-facing users.; L18: 4. Given an adult participant opens routine status, when they have assigned routine items, then they can view and act on their own assigned items, and admin-only controls remain hidden unless that participant has admin permission.; L19: 5. Given an active routine has prior activation snapshots (from Story 3.12's ring buffer), when an admin opens the routine settings, then they can view the list of available snapshots (version, activation timestamp, activating user) and select one to roll back to.; L20: 6. Given an admin selects a previous snapshot to roll back to, when they confirm, then: the routine's active snapshot is replaced with the selected snapshot, the draft is set to match the rolled-back `flow_config`, a ledger entry typed `rollback` is written containing the source snapshot version and the replaced snapshot version, and an auto-simulation is enqueued for the restored draft version (Story 3.7c).

Nine stories were written before anyone asked "where is the auth layer?"; L39: - 2.91.1 was created because adversarial review of the completed 2.91 found four P0 security flaws — an unguarded production token endpoint, non-atomic refresh rotation, a stub provider that fails at runtime not startup, and non-durable session persistence.; L48: ### Root Cause 2: Integration wiring was specified but not verified; L53: - Story 2.7 (rule drafting) and 2.8 (rule versioning) delivered correct isolated implementations. Story 2.93 found that `BaselinePolicyEvaluator` never called them — the rule system was entirely inert at runtime.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/spec-fixtures-honour-database-url.md`
- Size: 5822 bytes
- Title: Spec: Remove SQLite from test fixtures — PostgreSQL-only via Docker
- Headings: # Spec: Remove SQLite from test fixtures — PostgreSQL-only via Docker | ## Intent | ## Boundaries & Constraints | ## Tasks & Acceptance | ## Design Notes | ## Verification
- Relevant keyword hits: 8
- Task preview: L18: - [ ] Add `docker-compose.test.yml` at repo root with a `postgres:16` service (port 5433 to avoid conflict with a dev PG on 5432) and a `homefront_test` database. Health check via `pg_isready`.; L19: - [ ] Add `make test-db-up` and `make test-db-down` targets to root `Makefile` that start/stop the test Docker Compose.; L20: - [ ] Add `.env.test` template (committed, no secrets) with `DATABASE_URL=postgresql+asyncpg://postgres:postgres@localhost:5433/homefront_test`.; L21: - [ ] `conftest.py` — Replace `_make_engine()` with a version that reads `os.environ["DATABASE_URL"]` and raises `RuntimeError` if unset or if the URL does not begin with `postgresql`. Remove `_create_tables()` raw SQL DDL entirely. Replace with `await asyncio.to_thread(Base.metadata.create_all, sync_engine)` (or equivalent async-safe approach) using the ORM models as the schema source.; L22: - [ ] `conftest.py` — Fixture teardown: drop all tables after each test session (or use schema isolation per test via `BEGIN`/`ROLLBACK` transaction wrapping).
- Key lines preview: L5: **Problem:** `conftest.py` hardcoded `sqlite+aiosqlite:///:memory:` for the `engine`/`session`/`client` fixtures, and two standalone test modules (`test_auth_flow.py`, `test_auth_cleanup_2_91_1.py`) created their own SQLite engines. This is being completely removed. SQLite is not used in production and there is no value in maintaining dual-database compatibility in the test suite — doing so hides PostgreSQL-specific failures (triggers, UUID defaults, row-level locking, `server_default`, `gen_ran; L7: **Approach:** All test fixtures require a real PostgreSQL connection via `DATABASE_URL`. No SQLite fallback. A Docker Compose file provides a local PostgreSQL instance for dev and CI. Tests that previously used SQLite in-memory now run against PostgreSQL using `metadata.create_all()` with the actual ORM models for schema setup. A Makefile target `make test-db-up` starts the container; `make test-db-down` tears it down. CI already has a PostgreSQL service container configured — no CI changes requ; L11: **Always:** `DATABASE_URL` must be set to a PostgreSQL connection string — no fallback. If `DATABASE_URL` is unset or points to a non-PostgreSQL URL, tests fail immediately with a clear error. All tests use the same fixture API (`engine`, `session`, `client`, `test_client_factory`). Schema is created via `Base.metadata.create_all(engine)` from the ORM models — hand-written DDL is removed entirely.; L13: **Never:** SQLite. No `sqlite`, `aiosqlite`, or `/:memory:` anywhere in test or fixture code. No `if dialect == 'sqlite'` branches. No hand-written `CREATE TABLE` DDL in test fixtures.; L22: - [ ] `conftest.py` — Fixture teardown: drop all tables after each test session (or use schema isolation per test via `BEGIN`/`ROLLBACK` transaction wrapping).; L38: The previous approach used hand-written `CREATE TABLE` statements with SQLite-specific constructs (`lower(hex(randomblob(4)))` for UUIDs, `datetime('now','utc')` for timestamps, `TEXT` columns for UUID types). These were fundamentally incompatible with what the production ORM models generate. The fix is to use `Base.metadata.create_all(engine)` exclusively — the SQLAlchemy models in `models.py` encode the correct PostgreSQL schema and are the single source of truth for test schema too.; L40: For transaction isolation between tests, wrap each test in a `BEGIN`/`ROLLBACK` savepoint using SQLAlchemy's `AsyncSession` with `expire_on_commit=False` and a rolled-back transaction at teardown. This gives test isolation without truncating tables between every test, and works correctly with PostgreSQL triggers and sequences.; L48: - `grep -r "sqlite\|aiosqlite\|/:memory:" services/cell/tests/` — expected: zero matches.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/1-1-atomic-index-writes-and-write-mutex.md`
- Size: 24198 bytes
- Title: Story 1.1: Atomic Index Writes and Write Mutex
- Status signals: done; done
- Headings: # Story 1.1: Atomic Index Writes and Write Mutex | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ### Current State — What Needs to Change | ### Reference Implementation — Atomic Write Pattern | ### WikiQuery Mutex Implementation
- Relevant keyword hits: 49
- Acceptance criteria preview: L13: 1. **Given** any index `save()` method is called **When** it writes to disk **Then** it uses the tmp→`os.replace` pattern: write to `NamedTemporaryFile` in the same directory, then `os.replace(tmp, target)`. No index `save()` method uses `open(path, 'w')` directly.; L15: 2. **Given** `WikiQuery` is initialized **When** the instance is created **Then** it maintains a `dict[str, threading.Lock]` keyed by the three incrementally-written index names: `"fulltext"`, `"vector"`, `"metadata"`. `backlinks` and `graph_edges` are **not** in `_index_locks` — they are derived/computed indexes written only by `IndexRebuildJob` during full rebuilds, never incrementally per `add_page()`/`remove_page()`. Their rebuild-time exclusivity is guaranteed by `IndexRebuildJob` holding all three WikiQuery locks (blocking all incremental writes) for the duration of the rebuild.; L17: 3. **Given** any index write is triggered via `WikiQuery` methods (`add_page`, `remove_page`) **When** the write begins **Then** the per-index lock is acquired before calling `save()` and released after — even if `save()` raises.; L19: 4. **Given** `IndexRebuildJob` runs its full rebuild sweep **When** it executes **Then** it acquires all index locks before starting the sweep and releases all after completion. It is the only code path that bypasses `WikiQuery` methods and directly calls index `save()` — all other callers go through `WikiQuery`. `IndexRebuildJob.execute()` must call `rebuild_from_pages()` and `save()` directly on each index instance; it must never call `WikiQuery.rebuild_indexes()` or `WikiQuery.save_indexes()`, which would attempt to re-acquire already-held non-reentrant locks and deadlock.; L21: 5. **Given** two daemon workers attempt to write the same index simultaneously **When** the second arrives at the lock **Then** it blocks until the first completes — no data race, no silent corruption.
- Task preview: L27: - [x] Fix atomic writes in all 5 JSON-based index `save()` methods (AC: 1, 6); L28: - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/fulltext.py` — `save()`; L29: - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/metadata.py` — `save()`; L30: - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/backlinks.py` — `save()`; L31: - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/graph_edges.py` — `save()`
- Key lines preview: L28:   - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/fulltext.py` — `save()`; L29:   - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/metadata.py` — `save()`; L30:   - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/backlinks.py` — `save()`; L31:   - [x] `src/llm_wiki/index/graph_edges.py` — `save()`; L35:   - [x] Rename `remove_document()` → `remove_document_in_memory()` — removes from in-memory state only, no disk write; L36:   - [x] Keep `remove_document()` as a wrapper: calls `remove_document_in_memory()` then `_save_index_to_disk()` — preserves existing caller behavior; L37:   - [x] Update `WikiQuery.remove_page()` to call `remove_document_in_memory()` instead of `remove_document()`, then save under lock; L44:   - [x] `add_page()` — call `save()` on all 3 indexes after in-memory update, each locked

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/1-12-sqlite-query-log.md`
- Size: 16706 bytes
- Title: Story 1.12: SQLite Query Log
- Status signals: done; done
- Headings: # Story 1.12: SQLite Query Log | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ### SQLite Schema and Index | # src/llm_wiki/query/log.py | ### SQLite Connection Pattern
- Relevant keyword hits: 31
- Acceptance criteria preview: L15: 1. **Given** any query is executed via MCP, REST, or CLI **When** it completes **Then** a row is appended to `wiki_system/state/query_log.db` with columns: `query_hash`, `query_text`, `depth`, `domains` (JSON array), `result_count`, `confidence_avg`, `timestamp` (FR63).; L17: 2. **Given** the query log write **When** executed in an async route **Then** it runs inside `asyncio.to_thread()` and the SQLite connection uses `check_same_thread=False`.; L19: 3. **Given** `query_log.db` does not yet exist **When** the first query is logged **Then** the database file and schema are created automatically — no manual migration step required.; L21: 4. **Given** the `query_log.db` schema **When** created **Then** it has a composite index on `(query_hash, timestamp)` to support efficient repeated-query analysis at scale.; L23: 5. **Given** the same query text is submitted multiple times **When** logged **Then** all rows share the same `query_hash` — computed as a deterministic hash of normalized (lowercased, stripped) query text.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/1-12.5-observability-pipeline.md`
- Size: 15181 bytes
- Title: Story 1.12.5: OpenTelemetry Observability Pipeline
- Headings: # Story 1.12.5: OpenTelemetry Observability Pipeline | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ### Design Decision: OTel vs Prometheus | ### Package Versions (latest stable as of 2026) | ### OTel Python SDK Quick Reference
- Relevant keyword hits: 27
- Acceptance criteria preview: L19: 1. **Given** the service is running **When** it starts **Then** the OTel SDK initializes: traces, metrics, and logs are configured with an OTLP exporter (gRPC or HTTP, configurable via `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL` or `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` env vars). The SDK uses a `BatchSpanProcessor` for traces — not a simple sync one — with reasonable defaults (5s delay, 512 max batch size) that keep memory bounded and network efficient.; L21: 2. **Given** FastAPI requests are served **When** auto-instrumentation is installed **Then** a trace/root-span is emitted per request using `opentelemetry-instrumentation-fastapi`. Each Grafana/Alloy trace shows HTTP method, route, latency, and status code. HTTP attributes follow OpenTelemetry semantic conventions (e.g., `http.request.method`, `http.response.status_code`).; L23: 3. **Given** `QueryLogStore` fails to initialize during lifespan startup **When** it fails **Then** an exception is recorded on the lifespan span via `span.record_exception()`, and a `wiki_query_log_init_failed` metric gauge is set to 1 so Grafana can alert on startup failures.; L25: 4. **Given** any exception occurs in `QueryLogStore.log()` and is caught **When** it is caught **Then** the existing `logger.error()` call is augmented with a counter increment: `query_log_write_failures_total` recorded via `opentelemetry.metrics.get_meter("llm_wiki").create_counter("query_log_write_failures_total")`. This counter surfaces in Grafana dashboards so query log write failure rate can be alerted on externally.; L27: 5. **Given** OTel SDK is configured for log correlation **When** a log line is emitted **Then** every log line emitted in production includes the OTel `trace_id` and `span_id` (injected via Python logging integration) so Grafana/Alloy LogQL or log filters can correlate logs with traces by `trace_id` or `span_id`.

- Relevant keyword hits: 23
- Acceptance criteria preview: L13: 1. **Given** a markdown document is ingested **When** claims are extracted from it **Then** each claim is tagged with one of: `extracted` (directly stated in source), `inferred` (derived from source by reasoning), or `ambiguous` (unclear provenance or conflicting signals).; L15: 2. **Given** a Claude Code session transcript is ingested **When** QA pairs and claims are extracted **Then** each claim carries a trust tag reflecting its origin — `extracted` for direct Q&A pairs, `inferred` for synthesized summaries.; L17: 3. **Given** a page is committed to the wiki **When** its frontmatter is written **Then** claims include their trust tags as part of the stored page metadata — the tags survive index rebuilds and are returned in `GET /v1/pages/{page_id}`.; L19: 4. **Given** the trust tagging logic **When** implemented **Then** it is deterministic and algorithmic — no LLM calls; the same source document always produces the same tags.; L21: 5. **Given** a claim with tag `ambiguous` **When** surfaced in query results or governance reports **Then** it is distinguishable from `extracted` and `inferred` claims — the tag is present in the result payload, not just in stored metadata.

- Relevant keyword hits: 5
- Acceptance criteria preview: L13: 1. **Given** a page is scored by `QualityScorer` **When** it has no source citation **Then** the citation factor is `0.0` and the page score drops significantly.; L15: 2. **Given** a page has claims in its frontmatter **When** scored **Then** claims with `trust_tag: "ambiguous"` reduce the confidence score (weight from architecture: `trust_tag: 0.2` of the overall score).; L17: 3. **Given** the architecture-specified confidence weights (`citation_presence: 0.4`, `trust_tag: 0.2`, `source_count: 0.2`, `backlink_count: 0.1`, `recency: 0.1`) **When** `llm_extraction` is false **Then** the trust_tag weight redistributes to `citation_presence` (0.4 → 0.6).; L19: 4. **Given** a page with confidence score below a configurable threshold (default: `0.3`) **When** it is submitted for promotion **Then** it is blocked or flagged with a reason.; L21: 5. **Given** quality scoring runs on all pages **When** complete **Then** the scoring is deterministic (no LLM calls), using only frontmatter fields and trust tags.

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-1-authority-scoring.md`
- Size: 6752 bytes
- Title: Story 3.1: Authority Scoring
- Status signals: review; review
- Headings: # Story 3.1: Authority Scoring | ## Story | ## Acceptance Criteria | ## Tasks / Subtasks | ## Dev Notes | ### Key Files to Touch | ### Architecture Alignment | ### What NOT to change
- Relevant keyword hits: 13
- Acceptance criteria preview: L16: 1. **Given** an `IndexRebuildJob` completes **When** it finishes rebuilding all indexes **Then** it computes an authority score for each page based on the count of cross-domain backlinks pointing to it.; L18: 2. **Given** a page is referenced by pages in multiple distinct domains **When** authority scores are computed **Then** cross-domain references contribute more weight than same-domain references — a page referenced from 3 domains scores higher than one referenced 3 times within a single domain.; L20: 3. **Given** a page has zero cross-domain backlinks **When** scored **Then** its `authority_score` field is `0.0` — not omitted; always present in frontmatter.; L22: 4. **Given** `GET /v1/pages/{page_id}` **When** called **Then** the response includes `authority_score` as a numeric field.; L24: 5. **Given** `POST /v1/query` results with authority scores **When** ranked **Then** the ranking algorithm uses `authority_score` as a secondary sort signal after confidence — higher-authority pages rank above equal-confidence lower-authority pages.
- Task preview: L30: - [x] Task 1: Implement `AuthorityScorer` in `src/llm_wiki/synthesis/authority.py` (AC: 1, 2, 3); L31: - [x] 1.1 `compute_authority_scores(wiki_root: Path) -> dict[str, float]` — takes wiki root, returns `{page_id: score}` for all pages; L32: - [x] 1.2 Load backlink index; for each page, count incoming backlinks grouped by domain of the referring page; L33: - [x] 1.3 Score formula: `score = sum(1.0 / log2(1 + same_domain_count)) + sum_cross_domain * 2.0` — diminishing returns for same-domain, 2x boost for cross-domain; L34: - [x] 1.4 Normalize all scores to 0.0–1.0 range (divide by max score across wiki)
- Key lines preview: L9: So that high-signal pages surface first in queries and cross-domain synthesis can identify the most trusted nodes in the knowledge graph.; L30: - [x] Task 1: Implement `AuthorityScorer` in `src/llm_wiki/synthesis/authority.py` (AC: 1, 2, 3); L50:   - [x] 4.6 Integration: test endpoint includes authority_score in response; L55: - `src/llm_wiki/synthesis/authority.py` — NEW: AuthorityScorer module; L56: - `src/llm_wiki/daemon/jobs/index_rebuild.py` — UPDATE: call authority scoring after rebuild; L57: - `src/llm_wiki/api/routers/pages.py` — UPDATE: include authority_score in page detail response; L58: - `src/llm_wiki/api/routers/query.py` — UPDATE: secondary sort by authority_score; L74: - Integration test through IndexRebuildJob to verify frontmatter update

### `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/3-4-synthesis-cache-high-value-query-pages.md`

- Acceptance criteria preview: L16: 1. **Given** the query log (`state/query_log.db`) contains at least `synthesis_cache_min_hits` occurrences of the same `query_hash` within the rolling 30-day window (`synthesis_cache_window_days` in `daemon.yaml`) **When** the synthesis cache daemon job runs **Then** it identifies that query as a cache candidate; default threshold is 5 hits / 30 days (configurable via `daemon.yaml`).; L18: 2. **Given** a query is identified as a cache candidate **When** the synthesis cache job processes it **Then** it creates a `kind: synthesis` wiki page with the synthesized answer, tagged with `source_query` set to the original query text (FR48, FR62).; L20: 3. **Given** a `kind: synthesis` page exists for a query **When** the same query is submitted via MCP or REST **Then** the synthesis page is returned as the top result — the cache hit is served before running a fresh synthesis.; L22: 4. **Given** a synthesis cache page **When** `GET /v1/pages/{page_id}` is called **Then** the response includes `kind: "synthesis"` and `source_query: "<original query text>"` — distinguishable from primary source pages in all list and query results (FR62).; L24: 5. **Given** the source pages underlying a synthesis cache entry are updated **When** the synthesis cache job next runs **Then** it regenerates the synthesis page to reflect the updated knowledge — stale cache entries are refreshed, not preserved indefinitely.

- Acceptance criteria preview: L16: 1. **Given** a page whose `updated_at` is older than the `staleness_threshold_days` configured in `domains.yaml` for its domain **When** the governance daemon job runs **Then** the page is moved to an `archive/` subdirectory within its domain folder and excluded from normal query results (FR50a).; L18: 2. **Given** an archived page **When** `GET /v1/pages/{page_id}` is called with its ID **Then** it is still retrievable — archive preserves the page, it is only excluded from default query/search scope.; L20: 3. **Given** `POST /v1/query` or `GET /v1/search` **When** called without explicit archive inclusion **Then** archived pages are excluded from results — the active knowledge context does not include stale archived content.; L22: 4. **Given** `llm-wiki govern archive <page-id>` **When** run **Then** the specified page is archived immediately regardless of its staleness — manual archive is not gated on the threshold (FR50b).; L24: 5. **Given** `llm-wiki govern archive <page-id>` for a page that is already archived **When** run **Then** it is idempotent — no error, no duplicate archive entry.

### `_bmad-output/planning-artifacts/prd.md`
- Size: 38729 bytes
- Title: Product Requirements Document — LLM Wiki
- Headings: # Product Requirements Document — LLM Wiki | ## Executive Summary | ### What Makes This Special | ## Project Classification | ## Success Criteria | ### User Success | ### Business Success | ### Technical Success
- Relevant keyword hits: 66
- Key lines preview: L12:   deployment: docker_cloud_per_household; L23: LLM Wiki is a self-maintaining, daemon-governed knowledge service for agentic AI systems. It runs as a Docker container and exposes MCP, REST, and CLI interfaces — making it as easy to integrate as a database. Any agent harness connects over MCP; initial targets include OpenClaw, Hermes-agent, Agent Zero, and Homefront (custom). Knowledge is authored, maintained, and governed automatically by a background daemon, not by humans.; L25: **Stack positioning:** LLM Wiki is a structured knowledge store, not a memory system. It operates alongside session-memory systems like Honcho, not instead of them. Honcho manages session context and conversational continuity; LLM Wiki manages compiled, governed domain knowledge that compounds over time. An agent harness uses both simultaneously for different purposes.; L27: The core shift from traditional RAG: knowledge is compiled once, maintained continuously, and queried with provenance. Agents receive pre-synthesized answers with confidence scores, source citations, and contradiction warnings instead of raw chunks. Every new source makes the wiki richer through cross-referencing, entity integration, and synthesis caching — knowledge compounds; it does not evaporate between sessions.; L29: This is a brownfield expansion of a working V1 codebase. The pivot from importable Python library to standalone Docker service removes all embedding overhead — no language lock-in, no version conflicts, no harness-specific integration work. Each household runs its own isolated instance on a dedicated cloud VM alongside Homefront and Honcho. The service is auth-free (VM-level isolation is the security boundary), and plain-text on disk (Obsidian-compatible markdown); the knowledge is always yours ; L45: The competitive moat is completeness: every peer project has one or two of these. LLM Wiki has all of them in a single local service.; L50: - **Domain:** ML/AI tooling — knowledge graph, algorithmic governance, daemon orchestration; L53: - **Deployment:** Docker, cloud per-household (dedicated VM), no auth (VM-level isolation), plain-text data files

End-to-end pipeline tests go in `tests/integration/`.; L127: Patch `LLMClient.complete` to return a canned JSON response in unit tests — never make real API calls. Integration tests that exercise the full pipeline may use a real LLM if `OPENAI_API_KEY` is set, but must be marked `@pytest.mark.integration` and skipped otherwise.; L179: ### Page kinds — always use the factory; L181: `PageFrontmatter` is the base class. Subtypes: `EntityFrontmatter` (kind: `entity`), `ConceptFrontmatter` (kind: `concept`), `SourceFrontmatter` (kind: `source`), `QAFrontmatter` (kind: `qa`). Always use `create_frontmatter(kind=..., ...)` from `models/page.py`. Never instantiate `PageFrontmatter` directly for a typed kind — bypassing the factory produces a page missing kind-specific fields (e.g., no `entity_type` on an entity page), which silently corrupts kind-based index filtering downstream
